You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi is standing on a multiplication table with infinitely many rows and columns.
The square (i,j) contains the integer i \times j. Initially, Takahashi is standing at (1,1).
In one move, he can move from (i,j) to either (i+1,j) or (i,j+1).
Given an integer N, find the minimum number of moves needed to reach a square that contains N.

-----Constraints-----
 - 2 \leq N \leq 10^{12}
 - N is an integer.

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N

-----Output-----
Print the minimum number of moves needed to reach a square that contains the integer N.

-----Sample Input-----
10

-----Sample Output-----
5

(2,5) can be reached in five moves. We cannot reach a square that contains 10 in less than five moves.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i×j
2. We start at position (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum moves to reach any cell that contains value N

## Key Insight

Since we start at (1,1) and can only move right or down, we need to find factors of N such that if N = a × b, then we need to move from (1,1) to (a,b).

- To reach (a,b) from (1,1):
  - We need to move down (a-1) times (from row 1 to row a)
  - We need to move right (b-1) times (from column 1 to column b)
  - Total moves = (a-1) + (b-1) = a + b - 2

So for any factor pair (a,b) where a×b = N, the number of moves is a + b - 2.

We want to minimize this value, so we need to minimize a + b where a×b = N.

## Mathematical Optimization

For a fixed product N, the sum a + b is minimized when a and b are as close as possible to √N.

This happens when a = floor(√N) and b = N//a, or when a = ceil(√N) and b = N//a.

Actually, we need to find the factor pair (a,b) where a×b = N and a + b is minimized, which occurs when a and b are closest to each other.

So we should:
1. Find all divisors of N around √N
2. For each divisor d of N, we can form a pair (d, N//d)
3. Calculate moves = d + (N//d) - 2
4. Return the minimum moves

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find the square root of N
2. Check all divisors from 1 to √N
3. For each divisor d, calculate the pair (d, N//d)
4. Calculate moves = d + (N//d) - 2
5. Return the minimum moves

## Optimization

Since N can be up to 10^12, we need to be efficient. We only need to check divisors up to √N, which is 10^6 in worst case.

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve(N):
    min_moves = float('inf')
    
    # Check all divisors up to sqrt(N)
    sqrt_n = int(math.isqrt(N))
    
    for d in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if N % d == 0:
            # d is a divisor, so N//d is also a divisor
            a, b = d, N // d
            moves = a + b - 2
            min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)
    
    return min_moves

# Read input
N = int(input())
print(solve(N))
```

Let me verify with the sample:
- N = 10
- Divisors: 1, 2, 5, 10
- Pairs: (1,10), (2,5), (5,2), (10,1)
- For (1,10): moves = 1 + 10 - 2 = 9
- For (2,5): moves = 2 + 5 - 2 = 5
- For (5,2): moves = 5 + 2 - 2 = 5
- For (10,1): moves = 10 + 1 - 2 = 9
- Minimum = 5 ✓

The solution matches the expected output.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i×j
2. We start at position (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach any cell that contains the number N
5. From (1,1), we need to reach some (i,j) such that i×j = N

## Key Insights

1. To reach a cell (i,j) where i×j = N, we need to make (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2 moves (since we start at (1,1))
2. We need to find the pair (i,j) such that i×j = N and i + j is minimized
3. The minimum moves will be when i + j is minimized for i×j = N

## Mathematical Analysis

For a given product N, we want to minimize i + j where i×j = N.

If we fix i, then j = N/i, and i + j = i + N/i.

The sum i + N/i is minimized when i is as close as possible to √N.

So the optimal solution is when i is the integer closest to √N, and j = N/i.

We need to check both floor(√N) and ceil(√N) to ensure we get the minimum sum.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Find the integer part of √N
2. Check the pairs (i, N/i) where i is around √N
3. For each valid i (where i divides N), calculate i + N/i
4. Return the minimum sum minus 2 (since we start at (1,1))

Actually, let me reconsider. The number of moves from (1,1) to (i,j) is (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2.

So we need to minimize i + j for i×j = N.

## Algorithm

1. Find all divisors of N
2. For each divisor i of N, we have j = N/i
3. The moves needed is i + j - 2
4. Return the minimum such value

But since N can be up to 10^12, we can't iterate through all divisors. We only need to check divisors up to √N.

## Optimized Algorithm

1. Iterate i from 1 to √N
2. If i divides N, then we have two pairs: (i, N/i) and (N/i, i)
3. For each valid pair, calculate moves = i + N/i - 2
4. Return the minimum moves

Wait, actually we only need to check each divisor once. For each i from 1 to √N, if i divides N, then we have a valid pair (i, N/i).

The moves for pair (i, N/i) is i + N/i - 2.

We need to find the minimum of i + N/i - 2 over all divisors i of N.

Since i + N/i is minimized when i is close to √N, we can check divisors near √N.

```python
import math

def solve(N):
    min_moves = float('inf')
    
    # We only need to check divisors up to sqrt(N)
    sqrt_n = int(math.isqrt(N))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if N % i == 0:
            j = N // i
            # Moves from (1,1) to (i,j) is (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2
            moves = i + j - 2
            min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)
    
    return min_moves

N = int(input())
print(solve(N))
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i×j
2. We start at (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach any cell that contains N

## Key Insight

Since we start at (1,1) and can only move right or down, we can only reach cells (i,j) where i ≥ 1 and j ≥ 1.

To reach a cell that contains N, we need i × j = N for some i,j ≥ 1.

The number of moves needed to go from (1,1) to (i,j) is (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2.

So for a given factorization N = i × j, the moves needed are i + j - 2.

We want to minimize i + j - 2 over all factor pairs (i,j) such that i × j = N.

## Optimization

To minimize i + j for a given product N, we want i and j to be as close as possible to √N.

The minimum value of i + j occurs when i and j are as close as possible to √N.

So we need to:
1. Find all divisors of N
2. For each divisor d, we have a pair (d, N/d)
3. Calculate moves = d + (N/d) - 2
4. Return the minimum of these values

## Efficient Approach

We don't need to check all divisors. We can iterate from 1 to √N, and for each divisor i found, we get a pair (i, N/i).

The minimum of i + (N/i) will occur when i is closest to √N.

## Algorithm

1. Initialize min_moves to infinity
2. Iterate i from 1 to √N
3. If N % i == 0, then we have a valid factorization (i, N//i)
4. Calculate moves = i + (N//i) - 2
5. Update min_moves if this is smaller
6. Return min_moves

## Edge Cases

Since N ≥ 2, we're guaranteed at least one factorization.

## Implementation

```python
import math

N = int(input())

min_moves = float('inf')

# Check all divisors up to sqrt(N)
sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(N))
for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
    if N % i == 0:
        j = N // i
        # Moves to go from (1,1) to (i,j) is (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2
        moves = i + j - 2
        min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)

print(min_moves)
```

Let me verify with the sample:
- N = 10
- Divisors: (1,10), (2,5), (5,2), (10,1)
- For (1,10): moves = 1 + 10 - 2 = 9
- For (2,5): moves = 2 + 5 - 2 = 5
- For (5,2): moves = 5 + 2 - 2 = 5
- For (10,1): moves = 10 + 1 - 2 = 9
- Minimum is 5 ✓

The algorithm works correctly. The time complexity is O(√N), which is acceptable for N up to 10^12.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i×j
2. We start at (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach a cell that contains N

## Key Insights

1. We need to find a pair (i,j) such that i×j = N
2. The number of moves from (1,1) to (i,j) is (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2
3. We need to minimize i + j - 2 for all factor pairs (i,j) where i×j = N
4. Since N = i×j, we have j = N/i, so we need to minimize i + N/i - 2

## Strategy

1. For each divisor i of N, we can reach N at position (i, N/i)
2. The cost is i + N/i - 2
3. To minimize this, we need to find the divisor i that minimizes i + N/i

## Optimization

For the expression i + N/i, the minimum occurs when i is as close as possible to √N. This is because for any positive numbers x and y where x×y = constant, x+y is minimized when x=y.

So we only need to check divisors around √N.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all divisors of N, but we only need to check up to √N
2. For each divisor i of N (where i ≤ √N), consider the pair (i, N/i)
3. Calculate the cost i + N/i - 2
4. Return the minimum cost

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve(n):
    min_moves = float('inf')
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    # Check all divisors up to sqrt(n)
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            # i is a divisor, so (i, n/i) is a valid pair
            j = n // i
            moves = i + j - 2
            min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)
    
    return min_moves

# Read input
n = int(input())
print(solve(n))
```

## Verification with Sample

For N = 10:
- Divisors: 1, 2, 5, 10
- Pairs: (1,10), (2,5), (5,2), (10,1)
- Costs: 1+10-2=9, 2+5-2=5, 5+2-2=5, 10+1-2=9
- Minimum is 5 ✓

The solution works correctly and efficiently handles large values of N up to 10^12 by only checking divisors up to √N.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i×j
2. We start at (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach any cell that contains N

## Key Insights

1. To reach a cell (i,j) that contains N, we need i×j = N
2. The number of moves to reach (i,j) from (1,1) is (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2
3. We need to find all factor pairs (i,j) such that i×j = N
4. For each factor pair, calculate the moves: i + j - 2
5. Return the minimum of all these values

## Optimization

Instead of finding all factor pairs, we only need to check divisors up to √N:
- For each divisor i of N where i ≤ √N, we get a pair (i, N/i)
- The moves for this pair is i + N/i - 2
- We also need to consider that i and N/i could be swapped

## Algorithm Steps

1. Initialize minimum moves to a large value
2. Iterate i from 1 to √N
3. If i divides N, we have two pairs: (i, N/i) and (N/i, i)
4. Calculate moves for each pair and update minimum
5. Return minimum moves

## Edge Cases

- Since we start at (1,1), we can't reach (0, something) or (something, 0)
- All valid moves come from proper factor pairs

## Example Walkthrough (N=10)

Factors of 10: (1,10), (2,5), (5,2), (10,1)

Moves:
- (1,10): 1 + 10 - 2 = 9
- (2,5): 2 + 5 - 2 = 5
- (5,2): 5 + 2 - 2 = 5
- (10,1): 10 + 1 - 2 = 9

Minimum = 5 ✓

## Implementation

```python
import math

N = int(input())

min_moves = float('inf')

# Check all divisors up to sqrt(N)
sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(N))
for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
    if N % i == 0:
        # i is a divisor, so we have pairs (i, N//i) and (N//i, i)
        j1 = N // i
        moves1 = i + j1 - 2
        moves2 = j1 + i - 2  # Same as moves1, just swapping
        
        min_moves = min(min_moves, moves1)

print(min_moves)
```

Wait, I made a mistake. The moves for pair (i, N/i) is i + (N/i) - 2, and for (N/i, i) is (N/i) + i - 2, which is the same value. So I don't need to calculate both.

The code above will work correctly.

```python
import math

N = int(input())

min_moves = float('inf')

# Check all divisors up to sqrt(N)
sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(N))
for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
    if N % i == 0:
        # For divisor i, we have pair (i, N//i)
        j = N // i
        moves = i + j - 2
        min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)

print(min_moves)
```

for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
    if N % i == 0:
        # For divisor i, we have pair (i, N//i)
        j = N // i
        moves = i + j - 2
        min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)

print(min_moves)

Given an integer N, find the minimum number of moves needed to reach a square that contains N.

 - 2 \leq N \leq 10^{12}
 - N is an integer.

N

Print the minimum number of moves needed to reach a square that contains the integer N.

-----Sample Input-----
10

-----Sample Output-----
5

(2,5) can be reached in five moves. We cannot reach a square that contains 10 in less than five moves.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position (1,1) on a multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i×j
2. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
3. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach any cell that contains value N
4. The moves from (1,1) to (i,j) requires (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2 moves

## Key Insight

We need to find factor pairs (a,b) of N such that a×b = N, and minimize (a + b - 2) for such pairs.

Since we start at (1,1), to reach (a,b) we need (a-1) + (b-1) = a + b - 2 moves.

So we want to minimize (a + b - 2) = (a + b) - 2.

This means we want to find factor pairs (a,b) of N such that a×b = N and a + b is minimized.

## Optimization

For a fixed product N, the sum a + b is minimized when a and b are as close as possible to each other (they should be close to √N).

So we only need to check factors of N up to √N.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all divisors of N up to √N
2. For each divisor d of N, we have a pair (d, N/d)
3. Calculate the sum d + (N/d) for each pair
4. Find the minimum sum
5. Answer = minimum_sum - 2

## Edge Cases

- N is a perfect square: we have divisor √N paired with itself
- N has many factors, but we only need to check up to √N

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve(N):
    min_sum = float('inf')
    
    # Check all divisors up to sqrt(N)
    sqrt_n = int(math.isqrt(N))
    
    for d in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if N % d == 0:
            # d is a divisor, so (d, N//d) is a valid pair
            a, b = d, N // d
            current_sum = a + b
            min_sum = min(min_sum, current_sum)
    
    return min_sum - 2

# Read input
N = int(input())
print(solve(N))
```

Let me verify with the sample:
- N = 10
- Divisors up to √10 ≈ 3.16: check 1, 2, 3
- 10 % 1 = 0 → pair (1,10), sum = 11
- 10 % 2 = 0 → pair (2,5), sum = 7  
- 10 % 3 ≠ 0 → skip
- Minimum sum = 7
- Answer = 7 - 2 = 5 ✓

The solution matches the expected output. The time complexity is O(√N), which is efficient enough for N up to 10^12.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i × j
2. We start at (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach a square that contains N
5. The answer is the minimum number of moves to reach any cell (i,j) such that i × j = N

## Key Insights

Since we start at (1,1) and can only move right or down, to reach cell (i,j):
- We need (i-1) down moves and (j-1) right moves
- Total moves = (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2

We need to find any pair (i,j) such that i × j = N, and minimize i + j - 2.

## Strategy

For a given N, we want to minimize i + j where i × j = N.

The minimum value of i + j for i × j = N occurs when i and j are as close as possible to √N.

So the optimal approach is:
1. Find the divisor of N that is closest to √N
2. The minimizing pair (i,j) will be the pair of divisors that are closest to each other
3. The minimum moves = i + j - 2

## Algorithm

1. Find all divisors of N by iterating from 1 to √N
2. For each divisor i, j = N/i
3. Calculate the moves for each pair (i,j): i + j - 2
4. Return the minimum moves

However, we don't need to check all divisors. We only need to check divisors around √N.

Actually, since we want to minimize i + j for i × j = N, and by AM-GM inequality, the minimum occurs when i and j are closest to √N.

So we can:
- Find the divisor i that is the closest to √N
- Then j = N/i
- Answer = i + j - 2

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve(N):
    # We want to minimize i + j where i * j = N
    # This happens when i and j are closest to sqrt(N)
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(N))
    
    # Check divisors around sqrt(N)
    min_moves = float('inf')
    
    # Check all divisors from 1 to sqrt(N)
    for i in range(1, int(math.sqrt(N)) + 1):
        if N % i == 0:
            j = N // i
            moves = i + j - 2
            min_moves = min(min_moves, moves)
    
    return min_moves

N = int(input())
print(solve(N))
```

Wait, let me verify with the sample:
- N = 10
- Divisors: (1,10) → 1+10-2 = 9 moves
- Divisors: (2,5) → 2+5-2 = 5 moves  
- Divisors: (5,2) → same as (2,5)
- Divisors: (10,1) → 10+1-2 = 9 moves

Minimum is 5, which matches the expected output.

The algorithm looks correct.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite multiplication table where cell (i,j) contains i × j
2. We start at (1,1)
3. We can only move right (i,j) → (i,j+1) or down (i,j) → (i+1,j)
4. We need to find the minimum number of moves to reach a cell that contains N
5. The optimal path will go from (1,1) to some (i,j) where i × j = N

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only move right or down, to reach cell (i,j) from (1,1), we need exactly (i-1) down moves and (j-1) right moves.
2. Total moves = (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2
3. We need i × j = N
4. We want to minimize i + j - 2, which means we want to minimize i + j

## Strategy

To minimize i + j given that i × j = N, we should make i and j as close as possible to √N.

For a given N, we need to find factors i and j such that:
- i × j = N
- i + j is minimized
- Total moves = i + j - 2

The sum i + j is minimized when i and j are closest to each other, which happens when one of them is as close as possible to √N.

So, we need to find the factor pair (i,j) of N where i ≤ j and i is as close as possible to √N.

## Algorithm

1. Find the largest factor of N that is ≤ √N
2. Once we have i, j = N/i
3. Total moves = i + j - 2

To find this efficiently:
- We only need to check factors up to √N
- The optimal factor i will be the largest divisor of N that is ≤ √N
- This gives us the factor pair (i, N/i) that minimizes i + j

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve(n):
    # We need to find factors i and j such that i * j = n
    # and minimize i + j - 2 (total moves)
    
    # The minimum sum i + j occurs when i and j are closest to each other
    # This happens when i is as close as possible to sqrt(n)
    
    # Find the largest factor of n that is <= sqrt(n)
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    # Start from sqrt_n and go downwards to find the largest factor
    # We only need to check divisors up to sqrt(n)
    for i in range(sqrt_n, 0, -1):
        if n % i == 0:
            j = n // i
            # i and j are factors with i * j = n
            # Total moves = (i-1) + (j-1) = i + j - 2
            return i + j - 2
    
    # This should never happen for valid n >= 2
    return 0

# Read input
n = int(input())
print(solve(n))
```

## Verification with Sample

For N = 10:
- √10 ≈ 3.16, so we check factors from 3 down
- 10 % 3 ≠ 0
- 10 % 2 = 0, so i = 2, j = 10/2 = 5
- Total moves = 2 + 5 - 2 = 5 ✓

The answer matches the sample output.

```python
import math

def solve(n):
    # Find the largest factor of n that is <= sqrt(n)
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    # Check from sqrt_n down to 1 to find the largest factor
    for i in range(sqrt_n, 0, -1):
        if n % i == 0:
            j = n // i
            return i + j - 2
    
    return 0

n = int(input())
print(solve(n))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

ICP city has an express company whose trucks run from the crossing S to the crossing T. The president of the company is feeling upset because all the roads in the city are one-way, and are severely congested. So, he planned to improve the maximum flow (edge disjoint paths) from the crossing S to the crossing T by reversing the traffic direction on some of the roads.

Your task is writing a program to calculate the maximized flow from S to T by reversing some roads, and the list of the reversed roads.



Input

The first line of a data set contains two integers N (2 \leq N \leq 300) and M (0 \leq M \leq {\rm min} (1\,000,\ N(N-1)/2)). N is the number of crossings in the city and M is the number of roads.

The following M lines describe one-way roads in the city. The i-th line (1-based) contains two integers X_i and Y_i (1 \leq X_i, Y_i \leq N, X_i \neq Y_i). X_i is the ID number (1-based) of the starting point of the i-th road and Y_i is that of the terminal point. The last line contains two integers S and T (1 \leq S, T \leq N, S \neq T, 1-based).

The capacity of each road is 1. You can assume that i \neq j implies either X_i \neq X_j or Y_i \neq Y_j, and either X_i \neq Y_j or X_j \neq Y_i.

Output

In the first line, print the maximized flow by reversing some roads. In the second line, print the number R of the reversed roads. In each of the following R lines, print the ID number (1-based) of a reversed road. You may not print the same ID number more than once.

If there are multiple answers which would give us the same flow capacity, you can print any of them.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with N crossings and M one-way roads
2. Each road has capacity 1
3. We can reverse some roads (change direction) to increase the maximum flow from S to T
4. We need to find the maximum possible flow after reversing some roads, and which roads to reverse

## Key Insight

This problem is essentially about finding the maximum flow in a directed graph where we can reverse edges. 

The key insight is that we can model this as finding the maximum flow from S to T in a graph where:
- Original edges go from X to Y
- We can also have edges from Y to X (by reversing the original edge)

So we create a new graph that includes both original directed edges and their reverses (with capacity 1 each).

## Solution Approach

1. Build a graph with all original edges and their reverses
2. Find the maximum flow from S to T using a standard max flow algorithm (like Ford-Fulkerson or Edmonds-Karp)
3. Track which edges were used in the flow (these are the roads that we need to reverse)

However, there's a subtlety: when we reverse an edge, we're effectively creating a new edge in the opposite direction. So we can think of it as:
- Original edge: X → Y (capacity 1)
- Reversed edge: Y → X (capacity 1)

So the maximum flow from S to T in this augmented graph will give us the maximum possible flow after reversing some roads.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Create an adjacency list with capacities
2. Add edges in both directions (original and reversed)
3. Use BFS to find augmenting paths (Edmonds-Karp algorithm)
4. Track which original edges are reversed (i.e., which edges are used in the flow when considered in reverse direction)

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, in our flow network:
- For original edge i from X to Y, we have edge X→Y with capacity 1
- For the reverse, we don't need to explicitly add edges - when we solve max flow, the algorithm will automatically find paths that go through reversed edges.

Actually, the standard approach for this problem is to:
1. Create a flow network with both original edges and their reverses
2. For each original edge from X to Y, add edge X→Y with capacity 1
3. For each original edge from X to Y, add edge Y→X with capacity 1 (this represents the reverse)
4. Find max flow from S to T
5. The flow value is our answer
6. For each edge in the residual graph, if there's flow from Y to X (reverse direction), then original edge X→Y should be reversed

## Implementation Strategy

I'll use Edmonds-Karp algorithm to find max flow:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    # Read input
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    graph = defaultdict(dict)  # graph[u][v] = capacity
    
    for i in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((x, y, i + 1))  # (start, end, original_id)
        # Add original edge
        graph[x][y] = graph[x].get(y, 0) + 1
        # Add reverse edge (we'll handle flow through residual graph)
    
    s, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Create residual graph - initialize with original capacities
    residual = defaultdict(dict)
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            residual[u][v] = graph[u][v]
    
    # Edmonds-Karp algorithm for max flow
    def bfs():
        parent = {}
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([s])
        visited.add(s)
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in residual[u]:
                if v not in visited and residual[u][v] > 0:
                    visited.add(v)
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == t:
                        return parent, True
        return parent, False
    
    max_flow = 0
    reversed_road_ids = []
    
    # Find augmenting paths
    while True:
        parent, found = bfs()
        if not found:
            break
            
        # Find minimum capacity in the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, residual[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            residual[u][v] -= path_flow
            residual[v][u] = residual[v].get(u, 0) + path_flow  # Add reverse edge
            v = u
        
        max_flow += path_flow
    
    # Now, find which original roads were reversed
    # An original road from x to y is reversed if there's flow from y to x in the residual graph
    # This means we need to check if there's positive flow in the reverse direction
    
    # Actually, we need to identify which original edges were used to create flow in reverse
    # We need to find the edges that are used in the residual graph from destination to source
    # But since we don't know which original edges were reversed, let's think differently.
    
    # For each original edge (x, y, id), we check if there's flow from y to x (reverse flow)
    # If yes, then edge (x,y) should be reversed
    
    reversed_road_ids = []
    for x, y, original_id in edges:
        # Check if there's flow from y to x
        # This means we need to see if residual[y][x] > 0
        if residual[y].get(x, 0) > 0:
            reversed_road_ids.append(original_id)
    
    # Output
    print(max_flow)
    print(len(reversed_road_ids))
    for rid in reversed_road_ids:
        print(rid)

solve()
```

Wait, I see an issue. In my residual graph, I'm storing both directions, but the flow from y to x represents reverse flow in the original edge. 

However, there's a problem with how I'm initializing the residual graph. Let me fix it.

Actually, the residual graph should represent the original graph plus the reverse edges. For each original edge, we add both directions.

But I think my approach is fundamentally sound.

Let me revise and simplify:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    # residual[u][v] = capacity available from u to v
    residual = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    
    for i in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((x, y, i + 1))
        residual[x][y] += 1  # original edge capacity 1
    
    s, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Edmonds-Karp algorithm
    def bfs():
        parent = {}
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([s])
        visited.add(s)
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in residual[u]:
                if v not in visited and residual[u][v] > 0:
                    visited.add(v)
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == t:
                        return parent, True
        return parent, False
    
    max_flow = 0
    reversed_road_ids = []
    
    while True:
        parent, found = bfs()
        if not found:
            break
            
        # Find the minimum capacity on the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        curr = t
        while curr != s:
            prev = parent[curr]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, residual[prev][curr])
            curr = prev
        
        # Update residual capacities
        curr = t
        while curr != s:
            prev = parent[curr]
            residual[prev][curr] -= path_flow
            residual[curr][prev] += path_flow  # This creates reverse edge
            curr = prev
        
        max_flow += path_flow
    
    # Check which original roads are reversed
    # Original road from x to y is reversed if there's flow from y to x
    reversed_road_ids = []
    for x, y, original_id in edges:
        if residual[y][x] > 0:
            reversed_road_ids.append(original_id)
    
    print(max_flow)
    print(len(reversed_road_ids))
    for rid in reversed_road_ids:
        print(rid)

solve()
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with N nodes and M directed edges, each with capacity 1
2. We want to maximize the flow from source S to sink T by reversing some edges
3. When we reverse an edge, it changes direction (from X→Y becomes Y→X)
4. We need to find the maximum possible flow and the set of roads to reverse

## Key Insight

This is essentially a maximum flow problem where we can reverse edges. The key insight is that we can model this as a flow network where:
- Original edges go from X to Y with capacity 1
- Reversed edges go from Y to X with capacity 1 
- We can choose which edges to reverse to maximize flow from S to T

However, there's a more elegant approach: think of it as finding edge-disjoint paths in a modified graph. When we reverse an edge, we're essentially adding a reverse edge.

The maximum flow in this scenario is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths from S to T, where each path can use:
- Original edges (X → Y)
- Reversed edges (which were originally Y → X)

This is equivalent to finding the maximum flow in a network where we can use both original and reverse directions.

## Solution Approach

1. Create a flow network where we have original edges and potential reverse edges
2. Use maximum flow algorithm (like Ford-Fulkerson or Edmonds-Karp)
3. The maximum flow will give us the answer
4. To find which edges were reversed, we need to track the flow on reverse edges

Actually, there's a simpler insight: we can treat this as finding the maximum flow in a network where we can use both original edges and reverse edges. The maximum flow from S to T in this network will be our answer.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Create a graph with N nodes
2. For each original edge (X, Y), add an edge from X to Y with capacity 1
3. Add reverse edges (Y to X) with capacity 1 (these represent the edges we can reverse)
4. Run max flow from S to T
5. The flow value is our answer
6. For each edge in the original graph, if the reverse edge (from Y to X) was used in the flow, then this original edge was reversed

Wait, let me reconsider. When we reverse an edge, we don't create a new edge - we change the direction. So we need to think differently.

Actually, the problem allows us to reverse any road, which means we can convert any edge (X→Y) into (Y→X). The maximum flow is the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths from S to T, where each path can use:
- Original edges: X → Y
- Reversed edges: Y → X (which means we reversed the original edge X → Y)

So we can model this as a flow network where:
- Original edges: X → Y with capacity 1
- Reverse edges: Y → X with capacity 1 (these represent the edges we reverse)

The flow will tell us how many edges we have effectively used in the paths. The edges that are used in reverse direction (Y → X) correspond to the original edges that we reversed.

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(graph, s, t, parent):
    visited = [False] * len(graph)
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(s)
    visited[s] = True
    parent[s] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in range(len(graph)):
            if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == t:
                    return True
    return False

def max_flow(graph, s, t):
    n = len(graph)
    parent = [-1] * n
    max_flow = 0
    
    while bfs(graph, s, t, parent):
        path_flow = float('inf')
        s_copy = s
        while s_copy != t:
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[parent[s_copy]][s_copy])
            s_copy = parent[s_copy]
        
        max_flow += path_flow
        
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow
            v = u
    
    return max_flow

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
for i in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((x, y))

s, t = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
s -= 1
t -= 1

# Create adjacency matrix for flow network
# We'll have original edges (x->y) and reverse edges (y->x)
# The graph is n x n
graph = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]

# Add original edges
for x, y in edges:
    x -= 1
    y -= 1
    graph[x][y] = 1  # Original edge x->y

# Add reverse edges (which we can reverse to get y->x)
# We'll use the reverse edges as potential flow paths
# But we don't need to add them initially - we'll use the flow algorithm to determine which ones are used

# Run max flow
max_flow_value = max_flow(graph, s, t)

# Now determine which original edges were reversed
# An edge (x,y) is reversed if the flow went from y to x in the reverse direction
# In our flow network, if we have a flow from y to x, it means we used the reverse edge
# which means we reversed the original edge (x,y)

reversed_edges = []

# For each original edge (x,y), check if there's flow from y to x
# In our flow network, we have a reverse edge y->x with capacity 1
# We need to check the residual graph

# Reset the graph to original state
graph = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
for x, y in edges:
    x -= 1
    y -= 1
    graph[x][y] = 1  # Original edge x->y

# Calculate max flow again to get residual graph
# But we need to track which reverse edges were used
# Let's create a copy of the graph with residual values

# Re-run max flow to get residual graph
residual = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
for x, y in edges:
    x -= 1
    y -= 1
    residual[x][y] = 1

# Run max flow to get residual graph
def max_flow_with_flow_tracking(graph, s, t):
    n = len(graph)
    parent = [-1] * n
    max_flow = 0
    
    while True:
        visited = [False] * n
        queue = deque()
        queue.append(s)
        visited[s] = True
        parent[s] = -1
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in range(n):
                if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                    visited[v] = True
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == t:
                        break
            if visited[t]:
                break
        else:
            break
            
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        max_flow += path_flow
        
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow
            v = u
    
    return max_flow, graph

# Run max flow with tracking
max_flow_value, residual_graph = max_flow_with_flow_tracking(residual, s, t)

# Now check which reverse edges were used (which means original edges were reversed)
reversed_edges = []
for i, (x, y) in enumerate(edges):
    x -= 1
    y -= 1
    # Check if there's flow from y to x (reverse direction)
    # In residual graph, if residual_graph[y][x] > 0, then flow went from y to x
    # This means we reversed edge (x,y)
    if residual_graph[y][x] > 0:
        reversed_edges.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index

print(max_flow_value)
print(len(reversed_edges))
for edge_id in reversed_edges:
    print(edge_id)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

ICP city has an express company whose trucks run from the crossing S to the crossing T. The president of the company is feeling upset because all the roads in the city are one-way, and are severely congested. So, he planned to improve the maximum flow (edge disjoint paths) from the crossing S to the crossing T by reversing the traffic direction on some of the roads.

Your task is writing a program to calculate the maximized flow from S to T by reversing some roads, and the list of the reversed roads.



Input

The first line of a data set contains two integers N (2 \leq N \leq 300) and M (0 \leq M \leq {\rm min} (1\,000,\ N(N-1)/2)). N is the number of crossings in the city and M is the number of roads.

The following M lines describe one-way roads in the city. The i-th line (1-based) contains two integers X_i and Y_i (1 \leq X_i, Y_i \leq N, X_i \neq Y_i). X_i is the ID number (1-based) of the starting point of the i-th road and Y_i is that of the terminal point. The last line contains two integers S and T (1 \leq S, T \leq N, S \neq T, 1-based).

The capacity of each road is 1. You can assume that i \neq j implies either X_i \neq X_j or Y_i \neq Y_j, and either X_i \neq Y_j or X_j \neq Y_i.

Output

In the first line, print the maximized flow by reversing some roads. In the second line, print the number R of the reversed roads. In each of the following R lines, print the ID number (1-based) of a reversed road. You may not print the same ID number more than once.

If there are multiple answers which would give us the same flow capacity, you can print any of them.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def bfs(graph, src, dst, parent):
    visited = [False] * (len(graph))
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(src)
    visited[src] = True
    parent[src] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == dst:
                    return True
    return False

def max_flow(graph, src, dst):
    n = len(graph)
    parent = [-1] * n
    total_flow = 0
    
    while bfs(graph, src, dst, parent):
        # Find minimum capacity along the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        s = dst
        while s != src:
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[parent[s]][s])
            s = parent[s]
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = dst
        while v != src:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow
            v = u
        
        total_flow += path_flow
    
    return total_flow

    # look at the residual graph to see which backward edges are created
    
    # Run max flow to get residual graph
    residual_graph = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for x, y in roads:
        x -= 1
        y -= 1
        residual_graph[x][y] = 1
    
    while True:
        parent = [-1] * n
        found, parent = bfs_with_path(residual_graph, s, t, parent)
        if not found:
            break
            
        # Find minimum capacity on path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, residual_graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual graph
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            residual_graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            residual_graph[v][u] += path_flow
            v = u
        
        flow += path_flow
    
    # Now, in residual graph, edges with positive residual in reverse direction mean
    # we used that edge in reverse direction
    # Specifically, if residual_graph[v][u] > 0, it means we used edge (u,v) in reverse direction
    
    # But which original road is that?
    # We need to find which road has endpoints (u,v) and is used in reverse direction
    
    # We'll go through all roads and check if the reverse edge has positive flow
    reverse_used = set()
    
    for i, (x, y) in enumerate(roads):
        x -= 1
        y -= 1
        # Check if we have flow from y to x in reverse direction
        # In residual graph, if residual_graph[y][x] > 0, we used road (x,y) in reverse
        if residual_graph[y][x] > 0:
            reverse_used.add(i + 1)  # 1-based index
    
    # Output
    print(flow)
    print(len(reverse_used))
    for road_id in sorted(reverse_used):
        print(road_id)
```

Wait, I see an issue. The algorithm above doesn't correctly track which edges are used in reverse direction. Let me fix this.

Actually, the standard max flow algorithm I'm using with residual graphs should work. When we add flow from u to v, we reduce residual from u to v and increase from v to u.

So after running the max flow, if residual_graph[v][u] > 0 for some u,v, it means we have "unused" capacity in the reverse direction, but actually that doesn't mean we used the edge in reverse.

I need to think differently.

Actually, when we process a path in the max flow algorithm, if we use an edge from x to y, it means we add flow from x to y. To have flow from y to x, we need to have originally had an edge from x to y, and then we use it in reverse.

The key insight is that if in the residual graph after max flow, there's a positive capacity from y to x, it means we have flow going from y to x, which means we reversed the edge (x,y).

But actually, when we add flow from x to y, we reduce the residual in x->y and increase in y->x. So if residual_graph[y][x] > 0 after max flow, it means we have flow that goes from y to x, which means we used the original edge (x,y) in reverse direction.

So my algorithm is correct.

Let me verify with the examples:

In the residual graph, we have:
- 1->2 capacity 1

So residual_graph[1][2] = 1 > 0, which means we have flow from 1->2.

We need to find which original road has flow in reverse direction.

For road (1,2): we have flow from 2 to 1, so we used it in reverse direction? 

Actually, for road (1,2), we have flow from 2 to 1, which is reverse direction.

Let me code it more carefully:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(graph, src, dst, parent):
    n = len(graph)
    visited = [False] * n
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(src)
    visited[src] = True
    parent[src] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in range(n):
            if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == dst:
                    return True
    return False

def solve():
    line = input().split()
    n = int(line[0])
    m = int(line[1])
    
    roads = []
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        roads.append((x, y))
    
    s, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Convert to 0-based indexing
    s -= 1
    t -= 1
    
    # Create residual graph: graph[i][j] is capacity from i to j
    graph = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Add original edges (forward direction)
    for x, y in roads:
        x -= 1
        y -= 1
        graph[x][y] = 1
    
    # Run max flow algorithm
    total_flow = 0
    # We'll run until no augmenting path found
    while True:
        parent = [-1] * n
        if not bfs(graph, s, t, parent):
            break
            
        # Find minimum capacity on path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # add reverse edge capacity
            v = u
        
        total_flow += path_flow
    
    # Find which roads are used in reverse direction
    # A road (x,y) is used in reverse if there's flow from y to x in the residual graph
    reversed_roads = []
    
    for i, (x, y) in enumerate(roads):
        x -= 1  # convert to 0-based
        y -= 1
        # After max flow, if there's positive flow from y to x, it means we used (x,y) in reverse
        if graph[y][x] > 0:
            reversed_roads.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index
    
    # Output
    print(total_flow)
    print(len(reversed_roads))
    for road_id in reversed_roads:
        print(road_id)

solve()
```

    n = len(graph)
    visited = [False] * n
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(src)
    visited[src] = True
    parent[src] = -1

                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == dst:
                    return True
    return False

    total_flow = 0

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

ICP city has an express company whose trucks run from the crossing S to the crossing T. The president of the company is feeling upset because all the roads in the city are one-way, and are severely congested. So, he planned to improve the maximum flow (edge disjoint paths) from the crossing S to the crossing T by reversing the traffic direction on some of the roads.

Your task is writing a program to calculate the maximized flow from S to T by reversing some roads, and the list of the reversed roads.



Input

The first line of a data set contains two integers N (2 \leq N \leq 300) and M (0 \leq M \leq {\rm min} (1\,000,\ N(N-1)/2)). N is the number of crossings in the city and M is the number of roads.

The following M lines describe one-way roads in the city. The i-th line (1-based) contains two integers X_i and Y_i (1 \leq X_i, Y_i \leq N, X_i \neq Y_i). X_i is the ID number (1-based) of the starting point of the i-th road and Y_i is that of the terminal point. The last line contains two integers S and T (1 \leq S, T \leq N, S \neq T, 1-based).

The capacity of each road is 1. You can assume that i \neq j implies either X_i \neq X_j or Y_i \neq Y_j, and either X_i \neq Y_j or X_j \neq Y_i.

Output

In the first line, print the maximized flow by reversing some roads. In the second line, print the number R of the reversed roads. In each of the following R lines, print the ID number (1-based) of a reversed road. You may not print the same ID number more than once.

If there are multiple answers which would give us the same flow capacity, you can print any of them.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with N crossings and M one-way roads
2. Each road has capacity 1
3. We can reverse some roads to increase the maximum flow from S to T
4. We need to find the maximum possible flow and which roads to reverse

## Key Insight

The key insight is that reversing roads allows us to create new paths from S to T. This is equivalent to finding augmenting paths in a modified graph where we can "use" roads in reverse direction.

The maximum flow from S to T is limited by the number of edge-disjoint paths. When we reverse some roads, we effectively create alternative paths.

## Approach

This problem can be solved using the concept of "maximum flow with edge reversals". Here's the strategy:

1. Create a flow network where we can traverse original roads in forward direction and reversed roads in backward direction
2. Since we start with a directed graph, we can think of it as having edges from X to Y with capacity 1
3. When we reverse a road, we get an edge from Y to X with capacity 1
4. We want to maximize the flow from S to T

- Original edges go from X to Y with capacity 1
- We can also have edges from Y to X (reverse edges) with capacity 1, but we need to decide which ones to use

## Algorithm

1. Create a graph with two types of edges:
   - Original edges (u,v) with capacity 1
   - Reverse edges (v,u) with capacity 1 (which we can use by reversing that road)

2. Find the maximum flow from S to T using a standard max flow algorithm.

3. To determine which roads are actually reversed, we need to track the flow on the reverse edges.

Wait, the reverse edge from v to u represents that we reversed the original road from u to v.

So if flow goes through edge (v,u), that means we reversed the original road (u,v).

Algorithm:
- Build graph with original edges and potential reverse edges
- Run max flow from S to T
- For each reverse edge that has flow, record that the original edge was reversed

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    import sys
    from collections import defaultdict, deque
    
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    
    # Parse input
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    edges = []
    index = 2
    for i in range(m):
        x = int(data[index])
        y = int(data[index + 1])
        index += 2
        edges.append((x, y))
    
    s = int(data[index])
    t = int(data[index + 1])
    
    # Build graph for max flow
    # Graph is represented as adjacency list with capacities
    # Each edge (u,v) has capacity 1, edge (v,u) has capacity 1 (reverse)
    
    # Use adjacency list to represent the graph
    # graph[u][v] = capacity of edge from u to v
    graph = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    
    # Add original edges
    for u, v in edges:
        graph[u][v] = 1
    
    # We can reverse any edge, so we can also use reverse edges
    # But we need to decide which ones to reverse - we'll see which ones actually carry flow
    
    # For max flow, we'll treat each edge and its reverse with capacity 1
    # But we don't need to explicitly add reverse edges - we can just use the graph with bidirectional capability
    
    # Actually, we need to implement max flow algorithm that can handle this
    # We'll use Edmonds-Karp (BFS based) algorithm
    
    def bfs():
        # BFS to find augmenting path from s to t
        parent = {}
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([s])
        visited.add(s)
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in graph[u]:
                if v not in visited and graph[u][v] > 0:
                    visited.add(v)
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == t:
                        return parent
        return None
    
    # Run max flow
    flow = 0
    reversed_roads = []
    
    # We need to track which edges are reversed
    # For each edge in the original list, track if it's reversed
    # Actually, in our graph, edge from v to u represents that we reversed edge (u,v)
    
    # We need to store additional information about which original edges are reversed
    # Let's keep a list of original edges and their reversed status
    
    # Modified approach: we'll use the max flow algorithm on the graph where:
    # - Original edge from u->v with capacity 1
    # - We can use reverse edge from v->u with capacity 1 (which means we reversed the original edge)
    
    # But we don't need to add the reverse edge explicitly - we can just consider the graph is bidirectional
    # Actually, we need to build the graph with both directions
    
    # Let's rebuild the graph with both directions
    # But we have to be careful - we can only reverse existing roads
    
    # Build graph with both directions
    # For each original edge (u,v), we have u->v and v->u available (v->u means we reversed the edge)
    
    # Create a new graph with bidirectional edges
    new_graph = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    
    for u, v in edges:
        new_graph[u][v] = 1
        new_graph[v][u] = 1  # This represents the reverse edge
    
    # Now run max flow from s to t in this graph
    # But note: each edge can only be used once, so capacity is 1
    
    # Edmonds-Karp algorithm
    def max_flow():
        total_flow = 0
        while True:
            parent = bfs()
            if parent is None:
                break
                
            # Find the minimum capacity on the path
            path_flow = float('inf')
            v = t
            while v != s:
                u = parent[v]
                path_flow = min(path_flow, new_graph[u][v])
                v = u
            
            # Update the residual capacities
            v = t
            while v != s:
                u = parent[v]
                new_graph[u][v] -= path_flow
                new_graph[v][u] += path_flow  # Reverse edge gets capacity
                v = u
            
            total_flow += path_flow
        
        return total_flow
    
    # Run the max flow
    total_flow = max_flow()
    
    # Now find which original edges were reversed
    # An original edge (u,v) was reversed if the reverse edge (v,u) has positive flow in the residual graph
    # Actually, when we process flow, if there's flow on (v,u) in the residual graph, it means we used it
    # So we need to check residual graph
    
    # Reset the graph to original state for checking the reversed edges
    # After running max flow, we need to determine which original edges have been "used" in reverse direction
    
    # Let's create a fresh graph to determine which reverse edges actually carry flow
    # In the residual graph at the end, if new_graph[v][u] > 0 for some edge (u,v) in original list,
    # then we reversed the edge from u to v.
    
    # But note: the residual graph has more edges. We need to find edges that were used in reverse direction.
    
    # Actually, we can just check the residual graph: if new_graph[v][u] > 0 and (u,v) was an original edge,
    # then we reversed the edge from u to v.
    
    # Build list of which original edges are reversed
    reversed_roads_idx = []
    
    # Make a copy of the residual graph
    residual = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    for u in new_graph:
        for v in new_graph[u]:
            residual[u][v] = new_graph[u][v]
    
    # Check each original edge
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        # Check if there's flow on the reverse edge (v,u)
        if residual[v][u] > 0:
            reversed_roads_idx.append(i + 1)  # 1-based indexing
    
    # The flow value is total_flow
    print(total_flow)
    print(len(reversed_roads_idx))
    for idx in reversed_roads_idx:
        print(idx)

solve()
```

Wait, there's a problem. The residual graph in our algorithm has already been modified, and we're using it in max_flow. But in the max_flow algorithm, the residual capacities are updated, so when we check residual[v][u], it reflects the actual residual capacity.

- In this case, we can't get flow from 2 to 1 because there's no path, but we have edge (2,1) which goes from 2 to 1.

Example 2:

- We have edge from 1->2. But we want flow from 2->1.

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    edges = []
    index = 2
    for i in range(m):
        x = int(data[index])
        y = int(data[index + 1])
        index += 2
        edges.append((x, y))
    
    s = int(data[index])
    t = int(data[index + 1])
    
    # Build a graph where we can use original edges and reverse edges
    # For each original edge (u,v), we have:
    # - edge u->v with capacity 1 (original direction)
    # - edge v->u with capacity 1 (reverse direction, which means we reverse the original edge)
    
    # Create residual graph with bidirectional edges (each with capacity 1)
    graph = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    
    for u, v in edges:
        graph[u][v] = 1  # original edge
        graph[v][u] = 1  # reverse edge (can be used if we reverse this road)
    
    # Edmonds-Karp algorithm for max flow
    def bfs():
        parent = {}
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([s])
        visited.add(s)
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in graph[u]:
                if v not in visited and graph[u][v] > 0:
                    visited.add(v)
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == t:
                        return parent
        return None
    
    total_flow = 0
    
    while True:
        parent = bfs()
        if parent is None:
            break
            
        # Find minimum capacity on the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = t
        while v != s:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # when we use reverse edge, it adds to reverse capacity
            v = u
        
        total_flow += path_flow
    
    # Find which original edges were reversed
    # An original edge (u,v) was reversed if the reverse edge (v,u) has positive flow
    # In the residual graph, if graph[v][u] > 0 and (u,v) was in original edges, then we reversed it
    
    reversed_roads = []
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        # Check if we used the reverse edge (v,u)
        if graph[v][u] > 0:
            reversed_roads.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index
    
    print(total_flow)
    print(len(reversed_roads))
    for road_id in reversed_roads:
        print(road_id)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

        graph[v][u] = 1  # reverse edge (can be used if we reverse this road)

    def bfs():

            v = u

            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # when we use reverse edge, it adds to reverse capacity

    # In the residual graph, if graph[v][u] > 0 and (u,v) was in original edges, then we reversed it
    
    reversed_roads = []
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        # Check if we used the reverse edge (v,u)
        if graph[v][u] > 0:
            reversed_roads.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index
    
    print(total_flow)
    print(len(reversed_roads))
    for road_id in reversed_roads:
        print(road_id)

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def max_flow(graph, source, sink, n):
    # Use BFS to find augmenting paths
    def bfs():
        parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([source])
        visited[source] = True
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in graph[u]:
                if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                    visited[v] = True
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == sink:
                        return parent
        return None
    
    flow = 0
    while True:
        parent = bfs()
        if parent is None:
            break
            
        # Find minimum residual capacity along the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow
            v = u
        
        flow += path_flow
    
    return flow

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((x, y))

s, t = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph with bidirectional edges of capacity 1
# We'll use adjacency list with residual capacities
graph = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
# Also keep track of original edges and which ones were reversed
original_edges = []
for i, (x, y) in enumerate(edges):
    original_edges.append((x, y))
    # Add edge from x to y
    graph[x][y] += 1
    # Add edge from y to x (reverse of edge x to y)
    graph[y][x] += 1

# Calculate maximum flow from s to t
max_flow_value = max_flow(graph, s, t, n)

# Now find which edges were reversed
# An edge (x, y) in original is reversed if there's flow from y to x
# But we need to identify which original edge corresponds to which flow

# We need to be careful: we added duplicate edges. We need to track the original
# Let's reconstruct the residual graph that shows which direction has flow

# Actually, let's create a new flow graph with proper tracking

# Rebuild with capacity 1 for each direction, and then run flow
graph2 = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))

for i, (x, y) in enumerate(edges):
    graph2[x][y] = 1
    graph2[y][x] = 1  # reverse edge

# Run max flow
flow_graph = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
for i, (x, y) in enumerate(edges):
    flow_graph[x][y] = 1
    flow_graph[y][x] = 1

# Run max flow algorithm
def bfs_flow(flow_graph, source, sink, parent):
    visited = [False] * (len(flow_graph) + 1)
    queue = deque([source])
    visited[source] = True
    parent[source] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in flow_graph[u]:
            if not visited[v] and flow_graph[u][v] > 0:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == sink:
                    return True
    return False

# We need to run the max flow with tracking
def max_flow_with_tracking(flow_graph, source, sink, n):
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    total_flow = 0
    # We'll do multiple iterations
    while True:
        # BFS to find augmenting path
        parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([source])
        visited[source] = True
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in range(1, n + 1):
                if not visited[v] and flow_graph[u][v] > 0:
                    visited[v] = True
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == sink:
                        break
            if visited[sink]:
                break
        
        if not visited[sink]:
            break
            
        # Calculate path flow
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, flow_graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual graph
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            flow_graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            flow_graph[v][u] += path_flow
            v = u
        
        total_flow += path_flow
    
    return total_flow, flow_graph

# Run max flow with tracking
total_flow, final_flow_graph = max_flow_with_tracking(flow_graph, s, t, n)

# Now find which original edges are reversed
# An original edge (x, y) is reversed if there's flow in direction y->x
reversed_edges = []
for i, (x, y) in enumerate(edges):
    # Check if flow from y to x is positive in final_flow_graph
    if final_flow_graph[y][x] > 0:
        # This means we're using the edge from y to x, which is the reverse of (x, y)
        reversed_edges.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index

# Output
print(total_flow)
print(len(reversed_edges))
for edge_id in reversed_edges:
    print(edge_id)
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def max_flow_with_path_tracking(graph, source, sink, n):
    # graph is a dictionary of dictionaries with capacities
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    total_flow = 0
    
    while True:
        # BFS to find augmenting path
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([source])
        visited[source] = True
        parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
        
        while queue:
            u = queue.popleft()
            for v in range(1, n + 1):
                if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                    visited[v] = True
                    parent[v] = u
                    queue.append(v)
                    if v == sink:
                        break
            if visited[sink]:
                break
        
        if not visited[sink]:
            break
            
        # Find minimum residual capacity along the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[u][v])
            v = u
        
        # Update residual capacities along the path
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # reverse edge capacity increases
            v = u
        
        total_flow += path_flow
    
    return total_flow, graph
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with N nodes and M directed edges (roads)
2. Each edge has capacity 1
3. We can reverse some roads (change direction) to maximize the flow from S to T
4. We need to find the maximum flow and which roads to reverse

## Key Insight

This is a flow problem where we can reverse edges. The key insight is that we can model this as a flow network where:
- We have original edges going from X to Y
- We can also have reversed edges going from Y to X (by reversing the original edge)
- We want to find the maximum flow from S to T

However, since each edge has capacity 1, we can think of it as a network where we have two possible directions for each road.

## Approach

This is essentially a maximum flow problem where:
- Original edge from u to v (with capacity 1)
- Reversed edge from v to u (with capacity 1)

We can model this as a standard max flow problem on a network where for each original road, we have both directions.

The maximum flow from S to T will give us the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths (including paths that use reversed edges).

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build a flow network where each original edge and its reverse have capacity 1
2. Run max flow algorithm (like Ford-Fulkerson or Dinic's) from S to T
3. The flow value is the maximum flow
4. To find which roads were reversed, we need to track the flow on each edge

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(graph, source, sink, parent):
    visited = [False] * len(graph)
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(source)
    visited[source] = True
    parent[source] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == sink:
                    return True
    return False

def max_flow(graph, source, sink):
    # graph is represented as adjacency list with capacity
    parent = [-1] * len(graph)
    max_flow = 0
    
    while bfs(graph, source, sink, parent):
        # Find minimum capacity on the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        s = sink
        while s != source:
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[parent[s]][s])
            s = parent[s]
        
        # Add flow to the path
        max_flow += path_flow
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # reverse edge gets capacity
            v = u
    
    return max_flow

def solve():
    # Read input
    line = input().split()
    n = int(line[0])
    m = int(line[1])
    
    roads = []
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        roads.append((x, y))
    
    s, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list for max flow
    # Index nodes from 1 to n, so we need n+1 nodes
    # Create a graph with n+1 nodes (1-indexed)
    graph = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    
    # Initialize graph with capacity 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        graph[i][i] = 0
    
    # Add original edges and their reverses
    # We'll treat each road as having both directions with capacity 1
    # But we need to build the residual graph properly
    # For roads (x,y), we add x->y with capacity 1, and y->x with capacity 0 initially
    
    # Create a complete graph with initial capacities
    for x, y in roads:
        graph[x][y] += 1  # Original edge from x to y
        graph[y][x] += 0  # Reverse edge from y to x (capacity 0 initially)
    
    # Find max flow
    max_flow_value = max_flow(graph, s, t)
    
    # Now we need to find which roads were actually reversed
    # We need to trace back the flow to see which edges were used in reverse direction
    
    # Build a new graph from the residual capacities
    # For each edge, if residual capacity in reverse direction is > 0, it means it was reversed
    
    # Create list to track reverse usage
    reversed_roads = []
    
    # For each road, check if we used the reverse direction
    # In the residual graph, if graph[y][x] > 0, then we used the reverse of edge (x,y)
    
    for i, (x, y) in enumerate(roads):
        # Check if the reverse edge y->x has positive capacity in residual graph
        # The reverse edge was originally y->x with capacity 0, so any current capacity means flow was added
        # In our max flow algorithm, we added flow to reverse edges when we used paths through them
        # So if graph[y][x] > 0, it means we used the reverse of this road
        
        current_capacity = graph[y][x]
        if current_capacity > 0:
            reversed_roads.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index
    
    # Output results
    print(max_flow_value)
    print(len(reversed_roads))
    for road_id in reversed_roads:
        print(road_id)

solve()
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(graph, source, sink, parent):
    visited = [False] * len(graph)
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(source)
    visited[source] = True
    parent[source] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        
        for v in range(len(graph)):
            if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == sink:
                    return True
    return False

def max_flow(graph, source, sink):
    # graph is adjacency matrix, size (n+1) x (n+1)
    parent = [-1] * (len(graph))
    max_flow = 0
    
    while bfs(graph, source, sink, parent):
        # Find minimum capacity on the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        s = sink
        while s != source:
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[parent[s]][s])
            s = parent[s]
        
        # Add flow to the path
        max_flow += path_flow
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # reverse edge gets capacity
            v = u
    
    return max_flow

def solve():
    # Read input
    line = input().split()
    n = int(line[0])
    m = int(line[1])
    
    roads = []
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        roads.append((x, y))
    
    s, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency matrix for max flow
    # Size (n+1) x (n+1), 1-indexed
    graph = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    # Add all edges with capacity 1
    for x, y in roads:
        # Original edge x->y
        graph[x][y] = 1
    
    # Find max flow from s to t
    max_flow_value = max_flow(graph, s, t)
    
    # Find which roads were reversed
    # A road (x,y) is reversed if we have flow from y to x
    # This happens when graph[y][x] > 0 in the residual graph
    reversed_roads = []
    
    for i, (x, y) in enumerate(roads):
        # Check if reverse edge y->x has positive residual capacity
        if graph[y][x] > 0:
            reversed_roads.append(i + 1)  # 1-based index
    
    # Output results
    print(max_flow_value)
    print(len(reversed_roads))
    for road_id in reversed_roads:
        print(road_id)

solve()
```

View Extracted Code 
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(graph, source, sink, parent):
    visited = [False] * len(graph)
    queue = deque()
    queue.append(source)
    visited[source] = True
    parent[source] = -1
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        
        for v in range(len(graph)):
            if not visited[v] and graph[u][v] > 0:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)
                if v == sink:
                    return True
    return False

def max_flow(graph, source, sink):
    # graph is adjacency matrix, size (n+1) x (n+1)
    parent = [-1] * (len(graph))
    max_flow = 0
    
    while bfs(graph, source, sink, parent):
        # Find minimum capacity on the path
        path_flow = float('inf')
        s = sink
        while s != source:
            path_flow = min(path_flow, graph[parent[s]][s])
            s = parent[s]
        
        # Add flow to the path
        max_flow += path_flow
        
        # Update residual capacities
        v = sink
        while v != source:
            u = parent[v]
            graph[u][v] -= path_flow
            graph[v][u] += path_flow  # reverse edge gets capacity
            v = u
    
    return max_flow

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

ICP city has an express company whose trucks run from the crossing S to the crossing T. The president of the company is feeling upset because all the roads in the city are one-way, and are severely congested. So, he planned to improve the maximum flow (edge disjoint paths) from the crossing S to the crossing T by reversing the traffic direction on some of the roads.

Your task is writing a program to calculate the maximized flow from S to T by reversing some roads, and the list of the reversed roads.



Input

The first line of a data set contains two integers N (2 \leq N \leq 300) and M (0 \leq M \leq {\rm min} (1\,000,\ N(N-1)/2)). N is the number of crossings in the city and M is the number of roads.

The following M lines describe one-way roads in the city. The i-th line (1-based) contains two integers X_i and Y_i (1 \leq X_i, Y_i \leq N, X_i \neq Y_i). X_i is the ID number (1-based) of the starting point of the i-th road and Y_i is that of the terminal point. The last line contains two integers S and T (1 \leq S, T \leq N, S \neq T, 1-based).

The capacity of each road is 1. You can assume that i \neq j implies either X_i \neq X_j or Y_i \neq Y_j, and either X_i \neq Y_j or X_j \neq Y_i.

If there are multiple answers which would give us the same flow capacity, you can print any of them.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with N crossings and M one-way roads
2. Each road has capacity 1
3. We can reverse some roads (change direction) to maximize the flow from S to T
4. We need to find the maximum possible flow and which roads to reverse

## Key Insight

This is a classic maximum flow problem with the ability to reverse edges. The key insight is that we can model this as a flow network where:
- Original edges go from u to v with capacity 1
- We can also add reverse edges from v to u with capacity 1 (representing the possibility of reversing the original edge)
- The maximum flow from S to T in this network will be our answer

## Algorithm

1. Create a flow network with all original edges and their reverse edges
2. Use a max flow algorithm (like Ford-Fulkerson or Dinic's) to find the maximum flow from S to T
3. The flow through reverse edges tells us which original edges we need to reverse

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build the graph with original edges and their reverse edges (each with capacity 1)
2. Use BFS to find augmenting paths (since all capacities are 1, we can use simple BFS)
3. For each path found, we record which original edge we're using in reverse
4. The number of reverse edges needed is the total flow

## Implementation
